========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying that out loud, but that’s basically what it is.

After I eat like that, I usually end up finding some bathroom in the dorm, or sometimes a quiet floor of the library if I’m already there, and I make myself throw up. Not because I’m just randomly nauseous or something. It’s more like I get this panic that the food is just sitting there in me and turning into weight.

And then I’ll start thinking about how the bathroom tiles in the dorm are always gross, which is stupid because that’s not really the point, but I’ll focus on that instead of what I’m doing. But… yeah. The fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I do it.

I mean… yeah, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIt’s weird because I can have a totally fine day, like I’ll go to class and talk to people and feel mostly normal, and then I’ll catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows or the glass by the dining hall, and if my stomach looks bigger or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like… the whole day just drops out from under me.\n\nThen I’m not thinking about whatever we talked about in class or anything I did right. I just feel like I failed at everything, and I don’t want anyone to look at me. I know that’s not really a normal way to react to a reflection, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to exercise to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that exactly. I mean, sometimes after eating like that I feel really… gross, I guess, and I’ll think about going to the gym or walking around campus, but I don’t know if it’s specifically “to burn off calories” or if it’s just me wanting to feel less stuck in my own body.\n\nAlso the gym here is weirdly intimidating depending on the time of day—like if the baseball guys are there taking up all the racks, I’ll just kind of hover and leave, which is embarrassing. So… I’m not sure. I think the thought is there sometimes, but I don’t know how much I actually follow through with it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying that out loud, but that’s basically what it is.\n\nAfter I eat like that, I usually end up finding some bathroom in the dorm, or sometimes a quiet floor of the library if I’m already there, and I make myself throw up. Not because I’m just randomly nauseous or something. It’s more like I get this panic that the food is just sitting there in me and turning into weight.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about how the bathroom tiles in the dorm are always gross, which is stupid because that’s not really the point, but I’ll focus on that instead of what I’m doing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… probably not? Or at least it wouldn’t feel the same.

It’s hard to separate it, because so much of it is about what I think I look like. Like I can have a totally fine day in class—answer something right, get through my work, whatever—and then I’ll walk past one of those dark dorm windows and catch my reflection. If my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like the whole day just flips. Like I failed at everything, not just eating.

And then I start not wanting people to see me, which sounds dramatic when I say it. I’ll even think about which entrance has fewer people, which is dumb because everyone’s just going to dinner or doing laundry. But in my head it feels like they can tell.

So yeah… if I really didn’t care about my weight or shape, I don’t think I’d feel that same need. That’s kind of the center of it, I guess.

It’s just been… a lot.\n\nDoctor: When you say you keep going until you feel like you can't stand it, are you eating a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Like, after studying at night, I’ll leave the dining hall and then still go back to my room and keep eating—burgers, cereal, peanut butter, random snacks, whatever I have around. My desk gets covered, and then the trash is just full of wrappers and containers, and I feel sick after. Like actually sick.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… disgusting, I guess. Like I’ve ruined the whole day. I can have an okay day in classes, even feel like I’m keeping up, but then if I’m walking back and catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows and my face looks… I don’t know, puffier, or my stomach looks bigger than the guys around me, it’s like none of the other stuff counts.\n\nIt just feels like I failed at everything. And then I don’t want anyone to see me. I’ll kind of keep my head down or avoid people, even if it’s stupid because they’re probably not even looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for the amount you've eaten by doing something to get rid of the calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t really like saying it, but after I eat like that I usually end up finding a bathroom somewhere—like in the dorm, or sometimes a quieter floor of the library—and I make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s like I can’t stop thinking that the food is just… sitting there, turning into weight. Which sounds stupid when I say it out loud. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to go wash my face or something, and then I’m in there doing that. I mean… yeah, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIt’s weird because I can have a totally fine day, like I’ll go to class and talk to people and feel mostly normal, and then I’ll catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows or the glass by the dining hall, and if my stomach looks bigger or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like… the whole day just drops out from under me.\n\nThen I’m not thinking about whatever we talked about in class or anything I did right. It’s more like I get this panic that the food is just sitting there in me and turning into weight.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about how the bathroom tiles in the dorm are always gross, which is stupid because that’s not really the point, but I’ll focus on that instead of what I’m doing. But… yeah. The fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t weigh myself in a way that makes sense, if that makes sense. Like sometimes I’ll check and then avoid it for a while, and the numbers kind of blur together.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I can’t tell if that’s actually my body or just me being in my head about it. Like the dorm laundry machines shrink everything anyway—one of my shirts came out weird last week and I got kind of stuck on that.\n\nSo… significant changes? I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not? Or at least it wouldn’t feel the same.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it, because so much of it is about what I think I look like. Like I can have a totally fine day in class—answer something right, get through my work, whatever—and then I’ll walk past one of those dark dorm windows and catch my reflection. If my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like the whole day just flips. Like I failed at everything, not just eating.\n\nAnd then I start not wanting people to see me, which sounds dramatic when I say it. I’ll even think about which entrance has fewer people, which is dumb because everyone’s just going to dinner or doing laundry. But in my head it feels like they can tell.\n\nSo yeah… if I really didn’t care about my weight or shape, I don’t think I’d feel that same need. That’s kind of the center of it, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came because eating has started feeling really out of control. Like, I’ll start and then it’s like I’m not fully there anymore—like something else is steering. I’ll be thinking, “Stop, just stop,” but I keep going anyway until I feel like I can’t stand it.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds weird. It’s just been… a lot.\n\nDoctor: When you say you keep going until you feel like you can't stand it, are you eating a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Like, after studying at night, I’ll leave the dining hall and then still go back to my room and keep eating—burgers, cereal, peanut butter, random snacks, whatever I have around. It’s way more than just, like, a big dinner.\n\nSometimes I don’t even know why I bought half of it. My desk gets covered, and then the trash is just full of wrappers and containers, and I feel sick after. Like actually sick.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… disgusting, I guess. Like I’ve ruined the whole day. I can have an okay day in classes, even feel like I’m keeping up, but then if I’m walking back and catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows and my face looks… I don’t know, puffier, or my stomach looks bigger than the guys around me, it’s like none of the other stuff counts.\n\nIt just feels like I failed at everything. And then I don’t want anyone to see me. I’ll kind of keep my head down or avoid people, even if it’s stupid because they’re probably not even looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for the amount you've eaten by doing something to get rid of the calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t really like saying it, but after I eat like that I usually end up finding a bathroom somewhere—like in the dorm, or sometimes a quieter floor of the library—and I make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s like I can’t stop thinking that the food is just… sitting there, turning into weight. Which sounds stupid when I say it out loud. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to go wash my face or something, and then I’m in there doing that. I try to be quiet about it because I’m scared someone will hear.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise more than what's typically expected for someone of your age and fitness level?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know what “more than expected” means, honestly. Like, I’m in college and people here are always going to the gym, running, doing intramurals, and some guys are just… intense about it. So I compare myself to that and I can’t tell.\n\nI guess I think about it a lot, but as far as whether I’m exercising more than normal… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’ll go because I feel like I should, but then I’ll get distracted by classes or end up not going, and then I’ll feel weird about that. Sorry, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not like a once-in-a-while thing, if I’m being honest. For most of this semester it’s been happening several times a week.\n\nI try not to count it exactly, because then it feels like I’m making some weird spreadsheet of how messed up I am, but… yeah. Looking back over the last few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it totally stopped. It’ll happen, and then I’ll tell myself I’m done with it, and then a few days later it happens again.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yeah, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIt’s weird because I can have a totally fine day, like I’ll go to class and talk to people and feel mostly normal, and then I’ll catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows or the glass by the dining hall, and if my stomach looks bigger or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like… the whole day just drops out from under me.\n\nThen I’m not thinking about whatever we talked about in class or anything I did right. I just feel like I failed at everything, and I don’t want anyone to look at me. I know that’s not really a normal way to react to a reflection, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to exercise to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that exactly. I mean, sometimes after eating like that I feel really… gross, I guess, and I’ll think about going to the gym or walking around campus, but I don’t know if it’s specifically “to burn off calories” or if it’s just me wanting to feel less stuck in my own body.\n\nAlso the gym here is weirdly intimidating depending on the time of day—like if the baseball guys are there taking up all the racks, I’ll just kind of hover and leave, which is embarrassing. So… I’m not sure. I think the thought is there sometimes, but I don’t know how much I actually follow through with it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I know that’s not really a normal way to react to a reflection, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to exercise to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that exactly. I mean, sometimes after eating like that I feel really… gross, I guess, and I’ll think about going to the gym or walking around campus, but I don’t know if it’s specifically “to burn off calories” or if it’s just me wanting to feel less stuck in my own body.\n\nAlso the gym here is weirdly intimidating depending on the time of day—like if the baseball guys are there taking up all the racks, I’ll just kind of hover and leave, which is embarrassing. So… I’m not sure. I think the thought is there sometimes, but I don’t know how much I actually follow through with it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying that out loud, but that’s basically what it is.\n\nAfter I eat like that, I usually end up finding some bathroom in the dorm, or sometimes a quiet floor of the library if I’m already there, and I make myself throw up. Not because I’m just randomly nauseous or something. It’s more like I get this panic that the food is just sitting there in me and turning into weight.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about how the bathroom tiles in the dorm are always gross, which is stupid because that’s not really the point, but I’ll focus on that instead of what I’m doing. But… yeah. The fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t weigh myself in a way that makes sense, if that makes sense. Like sometimes I’ll check and then avoid it for a while, and the numbers kind of blur together.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I can’t tell if that’s actually my body or just me being in my head about it. Like the dorm laundry machines shrink everything anyway—one of my shirts came out weird last week and I got kind of stuck on that.\n\nSo… significant changes? I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not? Or at least it wouldn’t feel the same.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it, because so much of it is about what I think I look like. Like I can have a totally fine day in class—answer something right, get through my work, whatever—and then I’ll walk past one of those dark dorm windows and catch my reflection. If my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like the whole day just flips. I’ll even think about which entrance has fewer people, which is dumb because everyone’s just going to dinner or doing laundry. But in my head it feels like they can tell.\n\nSo yeah… if I really didn’t care about my weight or shape, I don’t think I’d feel that same need. That’s kind of the center of it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. Like, I notice things, but I don’t know if they’re real changes or just me checking too much.\n\nSome days my jeans feel tighter and I get stuck thinking about it, but then other days they’re fine, and also the dryers in the dorm are awful so I can’t tell if it’s me or the clothes. I mean, I don’t really like saying it, but after I eat like that I usually end up finding a bathroom somewhere—like in the dorm, or sometimes a quieter floor of the library—and I make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s like I can’t stop thinking that the food is just… sitting there, turning into weight. Which sounds stupid when I say it out loud. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to go wash my face or something, and then I’m in there doing that. I try to be quiet about it because I’m scared someone will hear.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise more than what's typically expected for someone of your age and fitness level?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know what “more than expected” means, honestly. Like, I’m in college and people here are always going to the gym, running, doing intramurals, and some guys are just… intense about it. So I compare myself to that and I can’t tell.\n\nI guess I think about it a lot, but as far as whether I’m exercising more than normal… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’ll go because I feel like I should, but then I’ll get distracted by classes or end up not going, and then I’ll feel weird about that. Sorry, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not like a once-in-a-while thing, if I’m being honest. For most of this semester it’s been happening several times a week.\n\nI try not to count it exactly, because then it feels like I’m making some weird spreadsheet of how messed up I am, but… yeah. Looking back over the last few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it totally stopped. It’ll happen, and then I’ll tell myself I’m done with it, and then a few days later it happens again.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yeah, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIt’s weird because I can have a totally fine day, like I’ll go to class and talk to people and feel mostly normal, and then I’ll catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows or the glass by the dining hall, and if my stomach looks bigger or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like… the whole day just drops out from under me.\n\nThen I’m not thinking about whatever we talked about in class or anything I did right. I just feel like I failed at everything, and I don’t want anyone to look at me. I know that’s not really a normal way to react to a reflection, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to exercise to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that exactly. I mean, sometimes after eating like that I feel really… gross, I guess, and I’ll think about going to the gym or walking around campus, but I don’t know if it’s specifically “to burn off calories” or if it’s just me wanting to feel less stuck in my own body.\n\nAlso the gym here is weirdly intimidating depending on the time of day—like if the baseball guys are there taking up all the racks, I’ll just kind of hover and leave, which is embarrassing. So… I’m not sure. I think the thought is there sometimes, but I don’t know how much I actually follow through with it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying that out loud, but that’s basically what it is.\n\nAfter I eat like that, I usually end up finding some bathroom in the dorm, or sometimes a quiet floor of the library if I’m already there, and I make myself throw up. Not because I’m just randomly nauseous or something. It’s more like I get this panic that the food is just sitting there in me and turning into weight.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about how the bathroom tiles in the dorm are always gross, which is stupid because that’s not really the point, but I’ll focus on that instead of what I’m doing. But… yeah. The fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t weigh myself in a way that makes sense, if that makes sense. Like sometimes I’ll check and then avoid it for a while, and the numbers kind of blur together.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I can’t tell if that’s actually my body or just me being in my head about it. Like the dorm laundry machines shrink everything anyway—one of my shirts came out weird last week and I got kind of stuck on that.\n\nSo… significant changes? I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not? Or at least it wouldn’t feel the same.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it, because so much of it is about what I think I look like. Like I can have a totally fine day in class—answer something right, get through my work, whatever—and then I’ll walk past one of those dark dorm windows and catch my reflection. If my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like the whole day just flips. Like I failed at everything, not just eating.\n\nAnd then I start not wanting people to see me, which sounds dramatic when I say it. I’ll even think about which entrance has fewer people, which is dumb because everyone’s just going to dinner or doing laundry. But in my head it feels like they can tell.\n\nSo yeah… if I really didn’t care about my weight or shape, I don’t think I’d feel that same need. That’s kind of the center of it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. Like, I notice things, but I don’t know if they’re real changes or just me checking too much.\n\nSome days my jeans feel tighter and I get stuck thinking about it, but then other days they’re fine, and also the dryers in the dorm are awful so I can’t tell if it’s me or the clothes. I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way. So… noticeable fluctuations, maybe? But I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: After you make yourself throw up, do you usually try to eat very little for a while?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if there’s a “usually,” exactly. Sometimes I tell myself I should just not eat much after, but then I kind of lose track of what counts as “very little,” you know?\n\nLike, it depends on the day, and on what else is happening. If I have class or if my roommate is around, or if the dining hall has those weird pasta nights where everything feels heavy—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear pattern I can point to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it has, I think. I mean, I’m still going to class, mostly, but it’s not like it’s not affecting things.

I’ve stopped going to a lot of meals with people from my dorm, or even just hanging out when I know food might be involved, because I get scared they’ll notice how weird I’m being around it. Like, people will say “let’s grab dinner” and I’ll make up some reason I can’t, even if I don’t actually have anything to do. And then later I’m just in my room anyway, which is kind of stupid because I’m avoiding feeling awkward but then I feel worse.

School’s gotten messier too. Not exactly because the work is harder, but because whole nights get swallowed up by thinking through when I’m going to eat, or what happens after, or trying to feel normal again afterward. I’ll sit down with my laptop open and then suddenly it’s hours later and I’ve barely done anything.

So… yeah. It’s definitely getting in the way.

Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to say it’s the only thing, because that sounds kind of extreme, but it feels like it takes over everything.

Like I can have a decent day in classes, actually answer a question or get through a discussion without feeling totally stupid, and then I’ll walk back to the dorm and catch my reflection in the window. And if my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks… I don’t know, rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like none of the good stuff counts anymore.

Then I just feel like I failed at everything, even if nothing actually happened. And I don’t want anyone to see me. I’ll start thinking about whether people noticed, which is probably not even realistic, but it feels real in the moment. So… yeah, I guess it does feel like my body decides how okay I am.

Sometimes I’ll go because I feel like I should, but then I’ll get distracted by classes or end up not going, and then I’ll feel weird about that. Sorry, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not like a once-in-a-while thing, if I’m being honest. For most of this semester it’s been happening several times a week.\n\nI try not to count it exactly, because then it feels like I’m making some weird spreadsheet of how messed up I am, but… yeah. Looking back over the last few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it totally stopped. It’ll happen, and then I’ll tell myself I’m done with it, and then a few days later it happens again.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yeah, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIt’s weird because I can have a totally fine day, like I’ll go to class and talk to people and feel mostly normal, and then I’ll catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows or the glass by the dining hall, and if my stomach looks bigger or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like… the whole day just drops out from under me.\n\nThen I’m not thinking about whatever we talked about in class or anything I did right. I just feel like I failed at everything, and I don’t want anyone to look at me. I know that’s not really a normal way to react to a reflection, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to exercise to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that exactly. I mean, sometimes after eating like that I feel really… gross, I guess, and I’ll think about going to the gym or walking around campus, but I don’t know if it’s specifically “to burn off calories” or if it’s just me wanting to feel less stuck in my own body.\n\nAlso the gym here is weirdly intimidating depending on the time of day—like if the baseball guys are there taking up all the racks, I’ll just kind of hover and leave, which is embarrassing. So… I’m not sure. I think the thought is there sometimes, but I don’t know how much I actually follow through with it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying that out loud, but that’s basically what it is.\n\nAfter I eat like that, I usually end up finding some bathroom in the dorm, or sometimes a quiet floor of the library if I’m already there, and I make myself throw up. Not because I’m just randomly nauseous or something. It’s more like I get this panic that the food is just sitting there in me and turning into weight.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about how the bathroom tiles in the dorm are always gross, which is stupid because that’s not really the point, but I’ll focus on that instead of what I’m doing. But… yeah. The fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t weigh myself in a way that makes sense, if that makes sense. Like sometimes I’ll check and then avoid it for a while, and the numbers kind of blur together.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I can’t tell if that’s actually my body or just me being in my head about it. Like the dorm laundry machines shrink everything anyway—one of my shirts came out weird last week and I got kind of stuck on that.\n\nSo… significant changes? I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not? Or at least it wouldn’t feel the same.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it, because so much of it is about what I think I look like. Like I can have a totally fine day in class—answer something right, get through my work, whatever—and then I’ll walk past one of those dark dorm windows and catch my reflection. If my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like the whole day just flips. Like I failed at everything, not just eating.\n\nAnd then I start not wanting people to see me, which sounds dramatic when I say it. I’ll even think about which entrance has fewer people, which is dumb because everyone’s just going to dinner or doing laundry. But in my head it feels like they can tell.\n\nSo yeah… if I really didn’t care about my weight or shape, I don’t think I’d feel that same need. That’s kind of the center of it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. Like, I notice things, but I don’t know if they’re real changes or just me checking too much.\n\nSome days my jeans feel tighter and I get stuck thinking about it, but then other days they’re fine, and also the dryers in the dorm are awful so I can’t tell if it’s me or the clothes. I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way. So… noticeable fluctuations, maybe? But I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: After you make yourself throw up, do you usually try to eat very little for a while?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if there’s a “usually,” exactly. Sometimes I tell myself I should just not eat much after, but then I kind of lose track of what counts as “very little,” you know?\n\nLike, it depends on the day, and on what else is happening. If I have class or if my roommate is around, or if the dining hall has those weird pasta nights where everything feels heavy—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear pattern I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes and the thoughts and behaviors surrounding them been interfering with your daily life, such as your relationships, schoolwork, or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I think. I mean, I’m still going to class, mostly, but it’s not like it’s not affecting things.\n\nI’ve stopped going to a lot of meals with people from my dorm, or even just hanging out when I know food might be involved, because I get scared they’ll notice how weird I’m being around it. Like, people will say “let’s grab dinner” and I’ll make up some reason I can’t, even if I don’t actually have anything to do. And then later I’m just in my room anyway, which is kind of stupid because I’m avoiding feeling awkward but then I feel worse.\n\nSchool’s gotten messier too. Not exactly because the work is harder, but because whole nights get swallowed up by thinking through when I’m going to eat, or what happens after, or trying to feel normal again afterward. I’ll sit down with my laptop open and then suddenly it’s hours later and I’ve barely done anything.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to say it’s the only thing, because that sounds kind of extreme, but it feels like it takes over everything.\n\nLike I can have a decent day in classes, actually answer a question or get through a discussion without feeling totally stupid, and then I’ll walk back to the dorm and catch my reflection in the window. And if my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks… I don’t know, rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like none of the good stuff counts anymore.\n\nThen I just feel like I failed at everything, even if nothing actually happened. And I don’t want anyone to see me. I’ll start thinking about whether people noticed, which is probably not even realistic, but it feels real in the moment. So… yeah, I guess it does feel like my body decides how okay I am.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ll sit down with my laptop open and then suddenly it’s hours later and I’ve barely done anything.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to say it’s the only thing, because that sounds kind of extreme, but it feels like it takes over everything.\n\nLike I can have a decent day in classes, actually answer a question or get through a discussion without feeling totally stupid, and then I’ll walk back to the dorm and catch my reflection in the window. And if my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks… I don’t know, rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like none of the good stuff counts anymore.\n\nThen I just feel like I failed at everything, even if nothing actually happened. And I don’t want anyone to see me. I’ll start thinking about whether people noticed, which is probably not even realistic, but it feels real in the moment. So… yeah, I guess it does feel like my body decides how okay I am.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you make yourself throw up after eating almost every time you feel you've eaten too much?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compensatory_Behavior_Purging\nmanifestation: \"After I eat like that, I usually end up in a bathroom in the dorm or a quiet floor of the library making myself throw up because I feel terrified that the food is just sitting there turning into weight.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… after I eat like that, it’s really hard not to. I start panicking that it’s just sitting there, like it’s already turning into weight, and then I’ll end up in a bathroom somewhere.

Usually it’s the dorm bathroom, but sometimes I’ll go to a quieter floor in the library if I’m there studying—or pretending to study, I guess. The library has these floors where no one really goes except during finals, and… sorry, that’s not important.

But yeah. When I feel like I’ve eaten too much, I usually make myself throw up.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came because eating has started feeling really out of control. Like, I’ll start and then it’s like I’m not fully there anymore—like something else is steering. I’ll be thinking, “Stop, just stop,” but I keep going anyway until I feel like I can’t stand it.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds weird. It’s just been… a lot.\n\nDoctor: When you say you keep going until you feel like you can't stand it, are you eating a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Like, after studying at night, I’ll leave the dining hall and then still go back to my room and keep eating—burgers, cereal, peanut butter, random snacks, whatever I have around. It’s way more than just, like, a big dinner.\n\nSometimes I don’t even know why I bought half of it. My desk gets covered, and then the trash is just full of wrappers and containers, and I feel sick after. Like actually sick.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… disgusting, I guess. Like I’ve ruined the whole day. I can have an okay day in classes, even feel like I’m keeping up, but then if I’m walking back and catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows and my face looks… I don’t know, puffier, or my stomach looks bigger than the guys around me, it’s like none of the other stuff counts.\n\nIt just feels like I failed at everything. And then I don’t want anyone to see me. I’ll kind of keep my head down or avoid people, even if it’s stupid because they’re probably not even looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for the amount you've eaten by doing something to get rid of the calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t really like saying it, but after I eat like that I usually end up finding a bathroom somewhere—like in the dorm, or sometimes a quieter floor of the library—and I make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s like I can’t stop thinking that the food is just… sitting there, turning into weight. Which sounds stupid when I say it out loud. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to go wash my face or something, and then I’m in there doing that. I try to be quiet about it because I’m scared someone will hear.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise more than what's typically expected for someone of your age and fitness level?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know what “more than expected” means, honestly. Like, I’m in college and people here are always going to the gym, running, doing intramurals, and some guys are just… intense about it. So I compare myself to that and I can’t tell.\n\nI guess I think about it a lot, but as far as whether I’m exercising more than normal… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’ll go because I feel like I should, but then I’ll get distracted by classes or end up not going, and then I’ll feel weird about that. Sorry, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not like a once-in-a-while thing, if I’m being honest. For most of this semester it’s been happening several times a week.\n\nI try not to count it exactly, because then it feels like I’m making some weird spreadsheet of how messed up I am, but… yeah. Looking back over the last few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it totally stopped. It’ll happen, and then I’ll tell myself I’m done with it, and then a few days later it happens again.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yeah, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIt’s weird because I can have a totally fine day, like I’ll go to class and talk to people and feel mostly normal, and then I’ll catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows or the glass by the dining hall, and if my stomach looks bigger or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like… the whole day just drops out from under me.\n\nThen I’m not thinking about whatever we talked about in class or anything I did right. I just feel like I failed at everything, and I don’t want anyone to look at me. I know that’s not really a normal way to react to a reflection, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to exercise to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that exactly. I mean, sometimes after eating like that I feel really… gross, I guess, and I’ll think about going to the gym or walking around campus, but I don’t know if it’s specifically “to burn off calories” or if it’s just me wanting to feel less stuck in my own body.\n\nAlso the gym here is weirdly intimidating depending on the time of day—like if the baseball guys are there taking up all the racks, I’ll just kind of hover and leave, which is embarrassing. So… I’m not sure. I think the thought is there sometimes, but I don’t know how much I actually follow through with it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying that out loud, but that’s basically what it is.\n\nAfter I eat like that, I usually end up finding some bathroom in the dorm, or sometimes a quiet floor of the library if I’m already there, and I make myself throw up. Not because I’m just randomly nauseous or something. It’s more like I get this panic that the food is just sitting there in me and turning into weight.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about how the bathroom tiles in the dorm are always gross, which is stupid because that’s not really the point, but I’ll focus on that instead of what I’m doing. But… yeah. The fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t weigh myself in a way that makes sense, if that makes sense. Like sometimes I’ll check and then avoid it for a while, and the numbers kind of blur together.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I can’t tell if that’s actually my body or just me being in my head about it. Like the dorm laundry machines shrink everything anyway—one of my shirts came out weird last week and I got kind of stuck on that.\n\nSo… significant changes? I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not? Or at least it wouldn’t feel the same.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it, because so much of it is about what I think I look like. Like I can have a totally fine day in class—answer something right, get through my work, whatever—and then I’ll walk past one of those dark dorm windows and catch my reflection. If my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like the whole day just flips. Like I failed at everything, not just eating.\n\nAnd then I start not wanting people to see me, which sounds dramatic when I say it. I’ll even think about which entrance has fewer people, which is dumb because everyone’s just going to dinner or doing laundry. But in my head it feels like they can tell.\n\nSo yeah… if I really didn’t care about my weight or shape, I don’t think I’d feel that same need. That’s kind of the center of it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. Like, I notice things, but I don’t know if they’re real changes or just me checking too much.\n\nSome days my jeans feel tighter and I get stuck thinking about it, but then other days they’re fine, and also the dryers in the dorm are awful so I can’t tell if it’s me or the clothes. I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way. So… noticeable fluctuations, maybe? But I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: After you make yourself throw up, do you usually try to eat very little for a while?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if there’s a “usually,” exactly. Sometimes I tell myself I should just not eat much after, but then I kind of lose track of what counts as “very little,” you know?\n\nLike, it depends on the day, and on what else is happening. If I have class or if my roommate is around, or if the dining hall has those weird pasta nights where everything feels heavy—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear pattern I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes and the thoughts and behaviors surrounding them been interfering with your daily life, such as your relationships, schoolwork, or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I think. I mean, I’m still going to class, mostly, but it’s not like it’s not affecting things.\n\nI’ve stopped going to a lot of meals with people from my dorm, or even just hanging out when I know food might be involved, because I get scared they’ll notice how weird I’m being around it. Like, people will say “let’s grab dinner” and I’ll make up some reason I can’t, even if I don’t actually have anything to do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… definitely more than once a week. I mean, I almost wish it were just once a week, because then maybe it would feel like some weird bad night or something.

But for most of this semester it’s been several times a week. Like, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, this week I’m not doing that,” and then Tuesday or Wednesday it’s already happened, and then sometimes again later. It’s hard to keep track exactly because the weeks kind of blur together with classes and laundry and… I don’t know, the dining hall schedule gets stuck in my head too much.

But looking back over the last few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it stopped.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came because eating has started feeling really out of control. Like, I’ll start and then it’s like I’m not fully there anymore—like something else is steering. I’ll be thinking, “Stop, just stop,” but I keep going anyway until I feel like I can’t stand it.\n\nSorry, that probably sounds weird. It’s just been… a lot.\n\nDoctor: When you say you keep going until you feel like you can't stand it, are you eating a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. Like, after studying at night, I’ll leave the dining hall and then still go back to my room and keep eating—burgers, cereal, peanut butter, random snacks, whatever I have around. It’s way more than just, like, a big dinner.\n\nSometimes I don’t even know why I bought half of it. My desk gets covered, and then the trash is just full of wrappers and containers, and I feel sick after. Like actually sick.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… disgusting, I guess. Like I’ve ruined the whole day. I can have an okay day in classes, even feel like I’m keeping up, but then if I’m walking back and catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows and my face looks… I don’t know, puffier, or my stomach looks bigger than the guys around me, it’s like none of the other stuff counts.\n\nIt just feels like I failed at everything. And then I don’t want anyone to see me. I’ll kind of keep my head down or avoid people, even if it’s stupid because they’re probably not even looking.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for the amount you've eaten by doing something to get rid of the calories or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t really like saying it, but after I eat like that I usually end up finding a bathroom somewhere—like in the dorm, or sometimes a quieter floor of the library—and I make myself throw up.\n\nIt’s like I can’t stop thinking that the food is just… sitting there, turning into weight. Which sounds stupid when I say it out loud. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to go wash my face or something, and then I’m in there doing that. I try to be quiet about it because I’m scared someone will hear.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise more than what's typically expected for someone of your age and fitness level?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know what “more than expected” means, honestly. Like, I’m in college and people here are always going to the gym, running, doing intramurals, and some guys are just… intense about it. So I compare myself to that and I can’t tell.\n\nI guess I think about it a lot, but as far as whether I’m exercising more than normal… I’m not sure. Sometimes I’ll go because I feel like I should, but then I’ll get distracted by classes or end up not going, and then I’ll feel weird about that. Sorry, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not like a once-in-a-while thing, if I’m being honest. For most of this semester it’s been happening several times a week.\n\nI try not to count it exactly, because then it feels like I’m making some weird spreadsheet of how messed up I am, but… yeah. Looking back over the last few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it totally stopped. It’ll happen, and then I’ll tell myself I’m done with it, and then a few days later it happens again.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yeah, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIt’s weird because I can have a totally fine day, like I’ll go to class and talk to people and feel mostly normal, and then I’ll catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows or the glass by the dining hall, and if my stomach looks bigger or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like… the whole day just drops out from under me.\n\nThen I’m not thinking about whatever we talked about in class or anything I did right. I just feel like I failed at everything, and I don’t want anyone to look at me. I know that’s not really a normal way to react to a reflection, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to exercise to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that exactly. I mean, sometimes after eating like that I feel really… gross, I guess, and I’ll think about going to the gym or walking around campus, but I don’t know if it’s specifically “to burn off calories” or if it’s just me wanting to feel less stuck in my own body.\n\nAlso the gym here is weirdly intimidating depending on the time of day—like if the baseball guys are there taking up all the racks, I’ll just kind of hover and leave, which is embarrassing. So… I’m not sure. I think the thought is there sometimes, but I don’t know how much I actually follow through with it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying that out loud, but that’s basically what it is.\n\nAfter I eat like that, I usually end up finding some bathroom in the dorm, or sometimes a quiet floor of the library if I’m already there, and I make myself throw up. Not because I’m just randomly nauseous or something. It’s more like I get this panic that the food is just sitting there in me and turning into weight.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about how the bathroom tiles in the dorm are always gross, which is stupid because that’s not really the point, but I’ll focus on that instead of what I’m doing. But… yeah. The fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t weigh myself in a way that makes sense, if that makes sense. Like sometimes I’ll check and then avoid it for a while, and the numbers kind of blur together.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I can’t tell if that’s actually my body or just me being in my head about it. Like the dorm laundry machines shrink everything anyway—one of my shirts came out weird last week and I got kind of stuck on that.\n\nSo… significant changes? I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not? Or at least it wouldn’t feel the same.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it, because so much of it is about what I think I look like. Like I can have a totally fine day in class—answer something right, get through my work, whatever—and then I’ll walk past one of those dark dorm windows and catch my reflection. If my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like the whole day just flips. Like I failed at everything, not just eating.\n\nAnd then I start not wanting people to see me, which sounds dramatic when I say it. I’ll even think about which entrance has fewer people, which is dumb because everyone’s just going to dinner or doing laundry. But in my head it feels like they can tell.\n\nSo yeah… if I really didn’t care about my weight or shape, I don’t think I’d feel that same need. That’s kind of the center of it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. Like, I notice things, but I don’t know if they’re real changes or just me checking too much.\n\nSome days my jeans feel tighter and I get stuck thinking about it, but then other days they’re fine, and also the dryers in the dorm are awful so I can’t tell if it’s me or the clothes. I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way. So… noticeable fluctuations, maybe? But I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: After you make yourself throw up, do you usually try to eat very little for a while?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if there’s a “usually,” exactly. Sometimes I tell myself I should just not eat much after, but then I kind of lose track of what counts as “very little,” you know?\n\nLike, it depends on the day, and on what else is happening. If I have class or if my roommate is around, or if the dining hall has those weird pasta nights where everything feels heavy—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear pattern I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes and the thoughts and behaviors surrounding them been interfering with your daily life, such as your relationships, schoolwork, or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I think. I mean, I’m still going to class, mostly, but it’s not like it’s not affecting things.\n\nI’ve stopped going to a lot of meals with people from my dorm, or even just hanging out when I know food might be involved, because I get scared they’ll notice how weird I’m being around it. Like, people will say “let’s grab dinner” and I’ll make up some reason I can’t, even if I don’t actually have anything to do. And then later I’m just in my room anyway, which is kind of stupid because I’m avoiding feeling awkward but then I feel worse.\n\nSchool’s gotten messier too. Not exactly because the work is harder, but because whole nights get swallowed up by thinking through when I’m going to eat, or what happens after, or trying to feel normal again afterward. I’ll sit down with my laptop open and then suddenly it’s hours later and I’ve barely done anything.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to say it’s the only thing, because that sounds kind of extreme, but it feels like it takes over everything.\n\nLike I can have a decent day in classes, actually answer a question or get through a discussion without feeling totally stupid, and then I’ll walk back to the dorm and catch my reflection in the window. And I don’t want anyone to see me. I’ll start thinking about whether people noticed, which is probably not even realistic, but it feels real in the moment. So… yeah, I guess it does feel like my body decides how okay I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating almost every time you feel you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… after I eat like that, it’s really hard not to. Sorry, I don’t know if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period of time happen?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not like a once-in-a-while thing, if I’m being honest. For most of this semester it’s been happening several times a week.\n\nI try not to count it exactly, because then it feels like I’m making some weird spreadsheet of how messed up I am, but… yeah. Looking back over the last few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it totally stopped. It’ll happen, and then I’ll tell myself I’m done with it, and then a few days later it happens again.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yeah, I’m afraid of it.\n\nIt’s weird because I can have a totally fine day, like I’ll go to class and talk to people and feel mostly normal, and then I’ll catch my reflection in one of those dorm windows or the glass by the dining hall, and if my stomach looks bigger or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like… the whole day just drops out from under me.\n\nThen I’m not thinking about whatever we talked about in class or anything I did right. I just feel like I failed at everything, and I don’t want anyone to look at me. I know that’s not really a normal way to react to a reflection, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to exercise to burn off calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like that exactly. I mean, sometimes after eating like that I feel really… gross, I guess, and I’ll think about going to the gym or walking around campus, but I don’t know if it’s specifically “to burn off calories” or if it’s just me wanting to feel less stuck in my own body.\n\nAlso the gym here is weirdly intimidating depending on the time of day—like if the baseball guys are there taking up all the racks, I’ll just kind of hover and leave, which is embarrassing. So… I’m not sure. I think the thought is there sometimes, but I don’t know how much I actually follow through with it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself throw up after eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying that out loud, but that’s basically what it is.\n\nAfter I eat like that, I usually end up finding some bathroom in the dorm, or sometimes a quiet floor of the library if I’m already there, and I make myself throw up. Not because I’m just randomly nauseous or something. It’s more like I get this panic that the food is just sitting there in me and turning into weight.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about how the bathroom tiles in the dorm are always gross, which is stupid because that’s not really the point, but I’ll focus on that instead of what I’m doing. But… yeah. The fear of gaining weight is a big part of why I do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t weigh myself in a way that makes sense, if that makes sense. Like sometimes I’ll check and then avoid it for a while, and the numbers kind of blur together.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel different some days, but then I can’t tell if that’s actually my body or just me being in my head about it. Like the dorm laundry machines shrink everything anyway—one of my shirts came out weird last week and I got kind of stuck on that.\n\nSo… significant changes? I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to make yourself throw up after eating if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably not? Or at least it wouldn’t feel the same.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it, because so much of it is about what I think I look like. Like I can have a totally fine day in class—answer something right, get through my work, whatever—and then I’ll walk past one of those dark dorm windows and catch my reflection. If my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like the whole day just flips. Like I failed at everything, not just eating.\n\nAnd then I start not wanting people to see me, which sounds dramatic when I say it. I’ll even think about which entrance has fewer people, which is dumb because everyone’s just going to dinner or doing laundry. But in my head it feels like they can tell.\n\nSo yeah… if I really didn’t care about my weight or shape, I don’t think I’d feel that same need. That’s kind of the center of it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. Like, I notice things, but I don’t know if they’re real changes or just me checking too much.\n\nSome days my jeans feel tighter and I get stuck thinking about it, but then other days they’re fine, and also the dryers in the dorm are awful so I can’t tell if it’s me or the clothes. I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way. So… noticeable fluctuations, maybe? But I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: After you make yourself throw up, do you usually try to eat very little for a while?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if there’s a “usually,” exactly. Sometimes I tell myself I should just not eat much after, but then I kind of lose track of what counts as “very little,” you know?\n\nLike, it depends on the day, and on what else is happening. If I have class or if my roommate is around, or if the dining hall has those weird pasta nights where everything feels heavy—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear pattern I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes and the thoughts and behaviors surrounding them been interfering with your daily life, such as your relationships, schoolwork, or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I think. I mean, I’m still going to class, mostly, but it’s not like it’s not affecting things.\n\nI’ve stopped going to a lot of meals with people from my dorm, or even just hanging out when I know food might be involved, because I get scared they’ll notice how weird I’m being around it. Like, people will say “let’s grab dinner” and I’ll make up some reason I can’t, even if I don’t actually have anything to do. And then later I’m just in my room anyway, which is kind of stupid because I’m avoiding feeling awkward but then I feel worse.\n\nSchool’s gotten messier too. Not exactly because the work is harder, but because whole nights get swallowed up by thinking through when I’m going to eat, or what happens after, or trying to feel normal again afterward. I’ll sit down with my laptop open and then suddenly it’s hours later and I’ve barely done anything.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to say it’s the only thing, because that sounds kind of extreme, but it feels like it takes over everything.\n\nLike I can have a decent day in classes, actually answer a question or get through a discussion without feeling totally stupid, and then I’ll walk back to the dorm and catch my reflection in the window. And if my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks… I don’t know, rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like none of the good stuff counts anymore.\n\nThen I just feel like I failed at everything, even if nothing actually happened. And I don’t want anyone to see me. I’ll start thinking about whether people noticed, which is probably not even realistic, but it feels real in the moment. So… yeah, I guess it does feel like my body decides how okay I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating almost every time you feel you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… after I eat like that, it’s really hard not to. I start panicking that it’s just sitting there, like it’s already turning into weight, and then I’ll end up in a bathroom somewhere.\n\nUsually it’s the dorm bathroom, but sometimes I’ll go to a quieter floor in the library if I’m there studying—or pretending to study, I guess. The library has these floors where no one really goes except during finals, and… sorry, that’s not important.\n\nBut yeah. When I feel like I’ve eaten too much, I usually make myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after eating at least once a week?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than once a week. I mean, I almost wish it were just once a week, because then maybe it would feel like some weird bad night or something.\n\nBut for most of this semester it’s been several times a week. Like, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, this week I’m not doing that,” and then Tuesday or Wednesday it’s already happened, and then sometimes again later. It’s hard to keep track exactly because the weeks kind of blur together with classes and laundry and… I don’t know, the dining hall schedule gets stuck in my head too much.\n\nBut looking back over the last few months, I don’t think there’s really been a week where it stopped.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like I can have a totally fine day in class—answer something right, get through my work, whatever—and then I’ll walk past one of those dark dorm windows and catch my reflection. If my stomach looks bigger, or my face looks rounder than the guys walking near me, it’s like the whole day just flips. Like I failed at everything, not just eating.\n\nAnd then I start not wanting people to see me, which sounds dramatic when I say it. I’ll even think about which entrance has fewer people, which is dumb because everyone’s just going to dinner or doing laundry. But in my head it feels like they can tell.\n\nSo yeah… if I really didn’t care about my weight or shape, I don’t think I’d feel that same need. That’s kind of the center of it, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable weight fluctuations over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. Like, I notice things, but I don’t know if they’re real changes or just me checking too much.\n\nSome days my jeans feel tighter and I get stuck thinking about it, but then other days they’re fine, and also the dryers in the dorm are awful so I can’t tell if it’s me or the clothes. I haven’t been keeping track in any organized way. So… noticeable fluctuations, maybe? But I couldn’t say for sure.\n\nDoctor: After you make yourself throw up, do you usually try to eat very little for a while?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if there’s a “usually,” exactly. Sometimes I tell myself I should just not eat much after, but then I kind of lose track of what counts as “very little,” you know?\n\nLike, it depends on the day, and on what else is happening. If I have class or if my roommate is around, or if the dining hall has those weird pasta nights where everything feels heavy—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear pattern I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Have these eating episodes and the thoughts and behaviors surrounding them been interfering with your daily life, such as your relationships, schoolwork, or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I think. I mean, I’m still going to class, mostly, but it’s not like it’s not affecting things.\n\nI’ve stopped going to a lot of meals with people from my dorm, or even just hanging out when I know food might be involved, because I get scared they’ll notice how weird I’m being around it. Like, people will say “let’s grab dinner” and I’ll make up some reason I can’t, even if I don’t actually have anything to do. And then later I’m just in my room anyway, which is kind of stupid because I’m avoiding feeling awkward but then I feel worse.\n\nSchool’s gotten messier too. Not exactly because the work is harder, but because whole nights get swallowed up by thinking through when I’m going to eat, or what happens after, or trying to feel normal again afterward. I’ll sit down with my laptop open and then suddenly it’s hours later and I’ve barely done anything.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to say it’s the only thing, because that sounds kind of extreme, but it feels like it takes over everything.\n\nLike I can have a decent day in classes, actually answer a question or get through a discussion without feeling totally stupid, and then I’ll walk back to the dorm and catch my reflection in the window. Looking back over the last few months, I can’t really think of a week where it didn’t happen.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you make yourself throw up after eating almost every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compensatory_Behavior_Purging\nmanifestation: \"After I eat like that, I usually end up in a bathroom in the dorm or a quiet floor of the library making myself throw up because I feel terrified that the food is just sitting there turning into weight.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… almost every day, I think. Not literally every single day maybe, because sometimes I’m stuck somewhere or people are around, but… after I eat like that, it usually ends with me finding a bathroom.

Mostly it’s the dorm bathroom, but if I’m in the library I’ll go to one of those quiet floors where nobody really goes unless it’s finals week. I don’t even know why I know which ones are empty, like I’ve mapped it out or something. But I get this panic that the food is just sitting there, like it’s already turning into weight, and then I feel like I have to make myself throw up.
